Patients should be instructed to rise slowly from a sitting position, and to report syncope or changes in blood pressure or heart rate to their health care provider.
Terbutaline: (Moderate) The concomitant use of rasagiline and sympathomimetic agents was not allowed in clinical studies; therefore, caution is advised during concurrent use of rasagiline and respiratory adrenergic agents (e.g., the beta-agonists). Although sympathomimetic agents are contraindicated for use with traditional non-selective monoamine oxidase inhibitors (MAOIs), hypertensive reactions generally are not expected to occur during concurrent use with rasagiline because of the selective monoamine oxidase-B (MAO-B) inhibition of rasagiline at manufacturer recommended doses. However, the cardiovascular effects of beta-2 agonists may be potentiated by concomitant use of MAOIs. At least one case of hypertension occurred in a patient with previous episodes of high blood pressure who was receiving albuterol and selegiline, a selective MAOI related to rasagiline, concurrently. Close observation for such effects is prudent, particularly if beta-2 agonists are administered during or within 2 weeks of use of an MAOI.
Teriflunomide: (Moderate) Use caution when administering teriflunomide with rasagiline. In vivo data suggest that teriflunomide is a weak inducer of CYP1A2. Coadministration of teriflunomide with CYP1A2 substrates, such as rasagiline, may decrease rasagiline exposure and lead to efficacy reduction. If teriflunomide is discontinued in a patient taking either rasagiline, serum concentrations may increase. Monitor patients for increases in adverse effects, such as dyskinesia, hallucinations, or nausea. Dose adjustments may be required.
Tetrabenazine: (Contraindicated) Tetrabenazine use is contraindicated in patients who are receiving or have received a monoamine oxidase inhibitor, such as rasagiline, within the past 14 days. The major metabolites of tetrabenazine (alpha-dihydrotetrabenazine [HTBZ] and beta-HTBZ) are reversible inhibitors of vesicular monoamine transporter 2 (VMAT2). Inhibition of VMAT2 results in decreased uptake of monoamines into synaptic vesicles and depletion of monoamine stores.
Tetrahydrozoline: (Moderate) The concomitant use of rasagiline and sympathomimetics was not allowed in clinical studies; therefore, caution is advised during concurrent use of rasagiline and sympathomimetics including non-prescription nasal and ophthalmic decongestants. Although sympathomimetics are contraindicated for use with other non-selective monoamine oxidase inhibitors (MAOIs), hypertensive reactions generally are not expected to occur during concurrent use with rasagiline because of the selective monoamine oxidase-B (MAO-B) inhibition of rasagiline at manufacturer recommended doses. One case of elevated blood pressure has been reported in a patient during concurrent use of the recommended dose of rasagiline and ophthalmic tetrahydrozoline. It should be noted that the MAO-B selectivity of rasagiline decreases as the dose increases above the recommended daily dose and interactions with sympathomimetics may be more likely to occur at these higher doses.
Thiazide diuretics: (Moderate) Additive hypotensive effects may be seen when monoamine oxidase inhibitors (MAOIs) are combined with antihypertensives. Careful monitoring of blood pressure is suggested during concurrent therapy of MAOIs with diuretics. Patients should be instructed to rise slowly from a sitting position, and to report syncope or changes in blood pressure or heart rate to their health care provider.
Thioridazine: (Major) Due to opposing effects on central dopaminergic activity, phenothiazines and rasagiline may interfere with the effectiveness of each other. Avoid concurrent use if possible and consider an atypical antipsychotic to the phenothiazine, if appropriate. If coadministration cannot be avoided, monitor for changes in movement, moods, or behaviors.
Thiothixene: (Major) Due to opposing effects on central dopaminergic activity, thiothixene and rasagiline may interfere with the effectiveness of each other. Avoid concurrent use if possible and consider an atypical antipsychotic as an alternative to thiothixene. If coadministration cannot be avoided, monitor for changes in movement, moods, or behaviors.
Timolol: (Moderate) Additive hypotensive effects may be seen when monoamine oxidase inhibitors (MAOIs) are combined with antihypertensives. Careful monitoring of blood pressure is suggested during concurrent therapy of MAOIs with beta-blockers. Limited data suggest that bradycardia is worsened when MAOIs are administered to patients receiving beta-blockers. Although the sinus bradycardia observed was not severe, until more data are available, clinicians should use MAOIs cautiously in patients receiving beta-blockers. Patients should be instructed to rise slowly from a sitting position, and to report syncope or changes in blood pressure or heart rate to their health care provider.
Tiotropium; Olodaterol: (Moderate) The concomitant use of rasagiline and sympathomimetic agents was not allowed in clinical studies; therefore, caution is advised during concurrent use of rasagiline and respiratory adrenergic agents (e.g., the beta-agonists). Although sympathomimetic agents are contraindicated for use with traditional non-selective monoamine oxidase inhibitors (MAOIs), hypertensive reactions generally are not expected to occur during concurrent use with rasagiline because of the selective monoamine oxidase-B (MAO-B) inhibition of rasagiline at manufacturer recommended doses. However, the cardiovascular effects of beta-2 agonists may be potentiated by concomitant use of MAOIs. At least one case of hypertension occurred in a patient with previous episodes of high blood pressure who was receiving albuterol and selegiline, a selective MAOI related to rasagiline, concurrently. Close observation for such effects is prudent, particularly if beta-2 agonists are administered during or within 2 weeks of use of an MAOI.
Torsemide: (Moderate) Additive hypotensive effects may be seen when monoamine oxidase inhibitors (MAOIs) are combined with antihypertensives. Careful monitoring of blood pressure is suggested during concurrent therapy of MAOIs with diuretics. Patients should be instructed to rise slowly from a sitting position, and to report syncope or changes in blood pressure or heart rate to their health care provider.
Tradipitant: (Moderate) Monitor for CNS depression and warn patients against driving and other activities that require complete mental alertness if concomitant use of tradipitant with another medication associated with CNS depression is necessary. Concomitant use may increase the risk for CNS depression-related adverse effects.
traMADol: (Contraindicated) Rasagiline is contraindicated for use with tramadol due to the risk of serotonin syndrome. Serotonin syndrome is characterized by the rapid development of hyperthermia, hypertension, myoclonus, rigidity, autonomic instability, mental status changes (e.g., delirium or coma), and in rare cases, death. At least 14 days should elapse between the discontinuation of rasagiline and the initiation of tramadol.
Tramadol; Acetaminophen: (Contraindicated) Rasagiline is contraindicated for use with tramadol due to the risk of serotonin syndrome. Serotonin syndrome is characterized by the rapid development of hyperthermia, hypertension, myoclonus, rigidity, autonomic instability, mental status changes (e.g., delirium or coma), and in rare cases, death. At least 14 days should elapse between the discontinuation of rasagiline and the initiation of tramadol.
Trandolapril: (Moderate) Additive hypotensive effects may be seen when monoamine oxidase inhibitors (MAOIs) are combined with antihypertensives. Careful monitoring of blood pressure is suggested during concurrent therapy of MAOIs with angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitors (ACE inhibitors). Patients should be instructed to rise slowly from a sitting position, and to report syncope or changes in blood pressure or heart rate to their health care provider.
Trandolapril; Verapamil: (Moderate) Additive hypotensive effects may be seen when monoamine oxidase inhibitors (MAOIs) are combined with antihypertensives. Careful monitoring of blood pressure is suggested during concurrent therapy of MAOIs with angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitors (ACE inhibitors). Patients should be instructed to rise slowly from a sitting position, and to report syncope or changes in blood pressure or heart rate to their health care provider. (Moderate) Additive hypotensive effects may be seen when monoamine oxidase inhibitors (MAOIs) are combined with antihypertensives. Careful monitoring of blood pressure is suggested during concurrent therapy of MAOIs with calcium-channel blockers. Patients should be instructed to rise slowly from a sitting position, and to report syncope or changes in blood pressure or heart rate to their health care provider during concurrent use of an MAOI and a calcium-channel blocker.
Tranylcypromine: (Contraindicated) Rasagiline should not be used concurrently with or in rapid succession to other monoamine oxidase inhibitors (MAOIs). The combination or rapid succession of MAOI treatment could result in severe CNS or cardiovascular reactions, including hypertensive crises, hyperpyrexia, CNS excitation, delirium, tremor, seizures, coma, circulatory collapse or death. At least 14 days should elapse between the discontinuation of rasagiline and the initiation of another MAOI or the discontinuation of another MAOI and the initiation of rasagiline.
traZODone: (Major) The manufacturer of rasagiline recommends against concurrent use with antidepressants, including trazodone, or use of an antidepressant within 14 days of discontinuing rasagiline since serotonin syndrome has been reported in patients treated with antidepressants and rasagiline. If coadministration is necessary, inform patients of the possible increased risk and monitor for the emergence of serotonin syndrome. Discontinue rasagiline and trazodone and initiate symptomatic treatment if serotonin syndrome occurs.
Treprostinil: (Moderate) Additive hypotensive effects may be seen when monoamine oxidase inhibitors (MAOIs) are combined with antihypertensives or medications with hypotensive properties. Careful monitoring of blood pressure is suggested during concurrent therapy of MAOIs with vasodilators. Patients should be instructed to rise slowly from a sitting position, and to report syncope or changes in blood pressure or heart rate to their health care provider.
Triamterene: (Moderate) Additive hypotensive effects may be seen when monoamine oxidase inhibitors (MAOIs) are combined with antihypertensives. Careful monitoring of blood pressure is suggested during concurrent therapy of MAOIs with diuretics. Patients should be instructed to rise slowly from a sitting position, and to report syncope or changes in blood pressure or heart rate to their health care provider.
Triamterene; hydroCHLOROthiazide, HCTZ: (Moderate) Additive hypotensive effects may be seen when monoamine oxidase inhibitors (MAOIs) are combined with antihypertensives. Careful monitoring of blood pressure is suggested during concurrent therapy of MAOIs with diuretics. Patients should be instructed to rise slowly from a sitting position, and to report syncope or changes in blood pressure or heart rate to their health care provider. (Moderate) Additive hypotensive effects may be seen when monoamine oxidase inhibitors (MAOIs) are combined with antihypertensives. Careful monitoring of blood pressure is suggested during concurrent therapy of MAOIs with diuretics. Patients should be instructed to rise slowly from a sitting position, and to report syncope or changes in blood pressure or heart rate to their health care provider.
Triazolam: (Moderate) The CNS-depressant effects of MAOIs can be potentiated with concomitant administration of other drugs known to cause CNS depression including buprenorphine, butorphanol, dronabinol, THC, nabilone, nalbuphine, and anxiolytics, sedatives, and hypnotics. Use these drugs cautiously with MAOIs; warn patients to not drive or perform other hazardous activities until they know how a particular drug combination affects them. In some cases, the dosages of the CNS depressants may need to be reduced.
Tricyclic antidepressants: (Major) It is recommended to avoid concurrent use of rasagiline and antidepressants, including tricyclic antidepressants or related compounds (e.g., amoxapine, maprotiline). Severe CNS toxicity with hyperpyrexia has been reported during concurrent use of antidepressants and selective or non-selective MAOIs. During post-marketing use of rasagiline, non-fatal cases of serotonin syndrome have been reported during concomitant antidepressant administration. At least 2 weeks should elapse between stopping rasagiline treatment and beginning therapy with any tricyclic antidepressants or related compounds. Conversely, when discontinuing a tricyclic or related compound, it is advisable to wait the length of 4-5 half lives of the individual agent being discontinued prior to initiation with rasagiline.
Trifluoperazine: (Major) Due to opposing effects on central dopaminergic activity, phenothiazines and rasagiline may interfere with the effectiveness of each other. Avoid concurrent use if possible and consider an atypical antipsychotic to the phenothiazine, if appropriate. If coadministration cannot be avoided, monitor for changes in movement, moods, or behaviors.
Trihexyphenidyl: (Moderate) MAOIs exhibit secondary anticholinergic actions. Additive anticholinergic effects may be seen when MAOIs are used concomitantly with antimuscarinics. Clinicians should note that antimuscarinic effects might be seen not only on GI smooth muscle, but also on bladder function, the eye, and temperature regulation. Additive CNS effects are also possible when many of these drugs are combined with MAOIs.
Trimipramine: (Major) It is recommended to avoid concurrent use of rasagiline and antidepressants, including tricyclic antidepressants or related compounds (e.g., amoxapine, maprotiline). Severe CNS toxicity with hyperpyrexia has been reported during concurrent use of antidepressants and selective or non-selective MAOIs. During post-marketing use of rasagiline, non-fatal cases of serotonin syndrome have been reported during concomitant antidepressant administration. At least 2 weeks should elapse between stopping rasagiline treatment and beginning therapy with any tricyclic antidepressants or related compounds. Conversely, when discontinuing a tricyclic or related compound, it is advisable to wait the length of 4-5 half lives of the individual agent being discontinued prior to initiation with rasagiline.
Triprolidine: (Moderate) Concurrent use of monoamine oxidase inhibitors (MAOIs) and sedating H1-blockers (sedating antihistamines) may result in additive sedation, anticholinergic effects, or hypotensive reactions. Rasagiline may be less likely to produce these interactions than other MAOIs, due to MAO-B selectivity. However, consider alternatives therapy to antihistamines where possible. If alternative combinations are not available, these medications may be used together with close monitoring. Many non-prescription products for coughs, colds, allergy, hay fever or insomnia contain sedating antihistamines. Patients receiving rasagiline should be counseled that it is essential to consult their healthcare provider or pharmacist prior to the use of any non-prescription products. Patients should also be advised against driving or engaging in other activities requiring mental alertness until they know how this combination affects them.
Trospium: (Moderate) MAOIs exhibit secondary anticholinergic actions. Additive anticholinergic effects may be seen when MAOIs are used concomitantly with antimuscarinics. Clinicians should note that antimuscarinic effects might be seen not only on GI smooth muscle, but also on bladder function, the eye, and temperature regulation. Additive CNS effects are also possible when many of these drugs are combined with MAOIs.
Tryptophan, 5-Hydroxytryptophan: (Major) Avoid use together if possible. The combination of tryptophan supplements and rasagiline may lead to increased levels of serotonin and serotonin syndrome. Tryptophan is a serotonin precursor. Rasagiline is a MAO-B inhibitor. In the postmarketing period, potentially life-threatening serotonin syndrome has been reported in patients treated with selected serotonergic agents concomitantly with rasagiline. There are also reports of tryptophan and MAO inhibitor interactions in the literature.
Umeclidinium; Vilanterol: (Moderate) The concomitant use of rasagiline and sympathomimetic agents was not allowed in clinical studies; therefore, caution is advised during concurrent use of rasagiline and respiratory adrenergic agents (e.g., the beta-agonists). Although sympathomimetic agents are contraindicated for use with traditional non-selective monoamine oxidase inhibitors (MAOIs), hypertensive reactions generally are not expected to occur during concurrent use with rasagiline because of the selective monoamine oxidase-B (MAO-B) inhibition of rasagiline at manufacturer recommended doses. However, the cardiovascular effects of beta-2 agonists may be potentiated by concomitant use of MAOIs. At least one case of hypertension occurred in a patient with previous episodes of high blood pressure who was receiving albuterol and selegiline, a selective MAOI related to rasagiline, concurrently. Close observation for such effects is prudent, particularly if beta-2 agonists are administered during or within 2 weeks of use of an MAOI.
Valbenazine: (Major) Avoid the use of valbenazine with rasagiline if possible. Rasagiline is a selective inhibitor of MAO type B (MAO-B), which results in an increased bioavailability of dopamine in the brain, while the therapeutic effect of valbenazine is thought to occur through inhibition of dopamine release. Concomitant use of valbenazine and rasagiline may result in an attenuated treatment effect of valbenazine or rasagiline.
Valerian, Valeriana officinalis: (Major) Any substances that act on the CNS, including rasagiline may theoretically interact with valerian, Valeriana officinalis. The valerian derivative, dihydrovaltrate, binds at barbiturate binding sites; valerianic acid has been shown to inhibit enzyme-induced breakdown of GABA in the brain; the non-volatile monoterpenes (valepotriates) have sedative activity. Patients taking MAOIs should discuss the use of herbal supplements with their health care professional prior to consuming valerian; combinations should be approached with caution in the absence of clinical data.
Valsartan: (Moderate) Additive hypotensive effects may be seen when rasagiline is combined with angiotensin II receptor antagonists. Careful monitoring of blood pressure is suggested during coadministration. Patients should be instructed to rise slowly from a sitting position, and to report syncope or changes in blood pressure or heart rate to their health care provider.
Valsartan; hydroCHLOROthiazide, HCTZ: (Moderate) Additive hypotensive effects may be seen when monoamine oxidase inhibitors (MAOIs) are combined with antihypertensives. Careful monitoring of blood pressure is suggested during concurrent therapy of MAOIs with diuretics. Patients should be instructed to rise slowly from a sitting position, and to report syncope or changes in blood pressure or heart rate to their health care provider. (Moderate) Additive hypotensive effects may be seen when rasagiline is combined with angiotensin II receptor antagonists. Careful monitoring of blood pressure is suggested during coadministration. Patients should be instructed to rise slowly from a sitting position, and to report syncope or changes in blood pressure or heart rate to their health care provider.
Vemurafenib: (Major) Do not exceed a rasagiline dose of 0.5 mg once daily when coadministered with vemurafenib. Coadministration may result in increased rasagiline concentrations. Rasagiline is primarily metabolized by CYP1A2; vemurafenib is a moderate CYP1A2 inhibitor. When rasagiline was administered with a strong CYP1A2 inhibitor, the AUC of rasagiline increased by 83%.
Venlafaxine: (Major) It is recommended to avoid concurrent use of rasagiline and antidepressants, including serotonin norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors (SNRIs). Severe CNS toxicity with hyperpyrexia has been reported during concurrent use of antidepressants and selective or non-selective MAOIs. During post-marketing use of rasagiline, non-fatal cases of serotonin syndrome have been reported during concomitant antidepressant administration. Serotonin syndrome is characterized by rapid development of hyperthermia, hypertension, myoclonus, rigidity, autonomic instability, mental status changes (e.g., delirium or coma), and in rare cases, death. Serotonin syndrome, in its most severe form, can resemble neuroleptic malignant syndrome. At least 2 weeks should elapse between stopping rasagiline treatment and beginning therapy with any antidepressant. Conversely, when discontinuing the antidepressant, it is advisable to wait the length of 4 to 5 half lives of the individual agent being discontinued prior to initiation with rasagiline.
Verapamil: (Moderate) Additive hypotensive effects may be seen when monoamine oxidase inhibitors (MAOIs) are combined with antihypertensives. Careful monitoring of blood pressure is suggested during concurrent therapy of MAOIs with calcium-channel blockers. Patients should be instructed to rise slowly from a sitting position, and to report syncope or changes in blood pressure or heart rate to their health care provider during concurrent use of an MAOI and a calcium-channel blocker.
Vilazodone: (Major) Avoid concurrent use of rasagiline and antidepressants such as vilazodone if possible. Serotonin syndrome has been reported during concurrent use of antidepressants and selective or non-selective MAOIs. During post-marketing use of rasagiline, non-fatal cases of serotonin syndrome have been reported during concomitant antidepressant use. Serotonin syndrome is characterized by rapid development of hyperthermia, hypertension, myoclonus, rigidity, autonomic instability, mental status changes (e.g., delirium or coma), and in rare cases, death. Serotonin syndrome, in its most severe form, can resemble neuroleptic malignant syndrome.
Viloxazine: (Contraindicated) Concurrent use of viloxazine with rasagiline or within two weeks after discontinuing rasagiline is contraindicated due to the risk for hypertensive crisis. If use is necessary, do not exceed a rasagiline dose of 0.5 mg once daily when coadministered with viloxazine. Coadministration may also result in increased rasagiline concentrations. Rasagiline is primarily metabolized by CYP1A2; viloxazine is a strong CYP1A2 inhibitor. When rasagiline was administered with a strong CYP1A2 inhibitor, the AUC of rasagiline increased by 83%.
Vortioxetine: (Major) Due to the risk of serotonin syndrome, monoamine oxidase inhibitors (MAOIs) intended to treat psychiatric disorders are contraindicated for use with vortioxetine or within 21 days of discontinuing treatment with vortioxetine. Conversely, vortioxetine should not be initiated within 14 days of stopping an MAOI. The manufacturer of rasagiline does not contraindicate the combination but recommends avoiding use of antidepressants with rasagiline or within 14 days of discontinuing rasagiline. Serotonin syndrome is characterized by the rapid development of hyperthermia, hypertension, myoclonus, rigidity, autonomic instability, mental status changes (e.g., delirium or coma), and in rare cases, death. Serotonin syndrome, in its most severe form, can resemble neuroleptic malignant syndrome.
Xanomeline; Trospium: (Moderate) MAOIs exhibit secondary anticholinergic actions. Additive anticholinergic effects may be seen when MAOIs are used concomitantly with antimuscarinics. Clinicians should note that antimuscarinic effects might be seen not only on GI smooth muscle, but also on bladder function, the eye, and temperature regulation. Additive CNS effects are also possible when many of these drugs are combined with MAOIs.
Zafirlukast: (Major) Do not exceed a rasagiline dose of 0.5 mg once daily when coadministered with zafirlukast. Coadministration may result in increased rasagiline concentrations. Rasagiline is primarily metabolized by CYP1A2; zafirlukast is a strong CYP1A2 inhibitor. When rasagiline was administered with a strong CYP1A2 inhibitor, the AUC of rasagiline increased by 83%.
Zaleplon: (Moderate) The CNS-depressant effects of MAOIs can be potentiated with concomitant administration of other drugs known to cause CNS depression including buprenorphine, butorphanol, dronabinol, THC, nabilone, nalbuphine, and anxiolytics, sedatives, and hypnotics. Use these drugs cautiously with MAOIs; warn patients to not drive or perform other hazardous activities until they know how a particular drug combination affects them. In some cases, the dosages of the CNS depressants may need to be reduced.
Zileuton: (Moderate) Monitor for dopaminergic adverse effects during concurrent use of rasagiline and zileuton. Coadministration may result in increased rasagiline concentrations. A dose reduction of rasagiline may be necessary. Rasagiline is primarily metabolized by CYP1A2; zileuton is a weak CYP1A2 inhibitor.
Ziprasidone: (Moderate) Monitor for movement disorders, unusual changes in moods or behavior, and diminished effectiveness of the atypical antipsychotic or rasagiline during coadministration. Due to mutually opposing effects on dopamine, atypical antipsychotics and rasagiline may interfere with the effectiveness of each other. In general, atypical antipsychotics are less likely to interfere with rasagiline than traditional antipsychotics. The Beers Criteria recognize quetiapine and clozapine as exceptions to the general recommendation to avoid all antipsychotics in older adults with Parkinson's disease.
ZOLMitriptan: (Minor) Serotonin syndrome has been reported during co-administration of serotonin-receptor agonists and monoamine oxidase inhibitors (MAOIs). Since rasagiline selectively inhibit MAO-B at recommended doses, no interaction with zolmitriptan would be expected with normal prescription use. However, there is a decline in MAO-B selectivity with increasing doses. Therefore, high dose treatment with rasagiline may increase central serotonin levels through MAO-A inhibition. However, whether or not 5-HT1B/1D agonists such as zolmitriptan can cause serious cases of serotonin syndrome when used with serotonergic drugs has been debated since serotonin syndrome is thought to result primarily from 5-HT2A activation. In vitro data indicate that zolmitriptan is partially metabolized by MAO-A. In one drug interaction study, selegiline 10 mg/day for 1 week had no effect on the pharmacokinetics of zolmitriptan or its metabolite. Theoretically, use of high dose rasagiline or selegiline could increase systemic exposure to zolmitriptan through MAO-A inhibition.
Zolpidem: (Moderate) The CNS-depressant effects of MAOIs can be potentiated with concomitant administration of other drugs known to cause CNS depression including buprenorphine, butorphanol, dronabinol, THC, nabilone, nalbuphine, and anxiolytics, sedatives, and hypnotics. Use these drugs cautiously with MAOIs; warn patients to not drive or perform other hazardous activities until they know how a particular drug combination affects them. In some cases, the dosages of the CNS depressants may need to be reduced.
Zuranolone: (Major) Avoid the use of multiple sedating agents due to the risk for additive CNS depression. If use is necessary, consider a downward dosage adjustment of either or both medications, especially in patients with additional risk factors for sedation-related harm.

Adverse Reactions
  Headline
  Adverse Reactions
  General Information
  Rasagiline was administered to roughly 1361 patients during Phase 2 and 3 clinical trials. About 283 patients received rasagiline for at least 1 year, approximately 410 patients received rasagiline for at least 2 years, 116 patients received rasagiline for at least 3 years, and 245 patients received rasagiline for more than 3 years. The long-term safety profile appears to be similar to that observed with shorter duration exposure. In general the most common adverse events seen with the use of rasagiline in clinical trials include: loss of weight, orthostatic or postural hypotension, dry mouth, abnormal involuntary movement, and hallucination (see rasagiline adverse reaction text below for more detail). Many of these adverse events may be dose-related.In the double-blind, placebo-controlled trials conducted in patients receiving rasagiline as monotherapy, roughly 5% of the patients treated with rasagiline (n=149) discontinued treatment due to adverse events compared to 2% of patients who received placebo (n=151). In studies conducted in patients treated with adjunctive levodopa therapy, approximately 9% of patients treated with rasagiline 0.5 mg/day PO (n=164) and 7% of patients treated with rasagiline 1 mg/day PO (n=149) stopped treatment due to adverse events compared to 6% of the patients who received placebo (n=159). In both the TEMPO and PRESTO clinical trials, serious adverse events occurred more frequently in older (>= 70 years) than younger patients (= 5% and more than placebo included: weight loss (2—9% vs. 3% placebo/levodopa), diarrhea (5—7% vs. 4% placebo/levodopa, abdominal pain (2—5% vs. 1% placebo/levodopa), anorexia (2—5% vs. 1% placebo/levodopa), nausea/vomiting (nausea: 10—12% rasagiline vs. 8% placebo/levodopa; vomiting: 4—7% vs. 1% placebo/levodopa), constipation (4—9% vs. 5% placebo/levodopa) and xerostomia (2—6% vs. 3% placebo/levodopa). Rasagiline may be taken with food ; it is not known if this will help to lessen the incidence of adverse GI events.Other adverse events that occurred in >= 5% of patients (n=149) receiving rasagiline as monotherapy or adjunctive therapy (and were at least 1.5 times the incidence in the placebo arm) include: dyspnea (3—5% vs. 2% placebo), influenza syndrome, arthralgia (6—8% vs. 4% placebo/levodopa), depression, dyspepsia (4—5% vs. 4% placebo/levodopa), and falls. Rash (unspecified) has also been reported (3—6% vs. 3% placebo/levodopa).Other potentially clinically important treatment emergent adverse events that occurred in >= 2% of patients receiving rasagiline (and at a higher frequency than in the placebo group) include: gingivitis, headache (8—11% vs. 10% placebo/levodopa), hemorrhage, hernia, conjunctivitis, dystonic reaction, fever, infection (2—3% vs. 2% placebo), rhinitis, ecchymosis or bruises (2—5% vs. 3% placebo/levodopa, malaise, myasthenia, neck pain (musculoskeletal pain), sweating, tenosynovitis.Other events of potential clinical importance reported by 1% or more of patients treated with rasagiline 1 mg/day as adjunct to levodopa therapy, and at least as frequent as in the placebo group, in descending order of frequency include: skin carcinoma, anemia, albuminuria, amnesia, arthritis, bursitis, cerebrovascular accident or stroke, confusion, dysphagia, epistaxis, leg or muscle cramps, pruritus, skin ulcer. Specific rates of incidence are not available for these adverse events.Other adverse events that occurred frequently (in at least 1/100 patients) but without determination of causality to rasagiline include: asthenia, bundle-branch block, GI bleeding, abnormal gait, anxiety, hyperkinesis, hypertonia, peripheral neuropathy, tremor, cough, hematuria, urinary incontinence. Additional information detailing infrequent adverse events (defined as those occurring in at least 1/100 to 1/1000 patients) and rare adverse events (defined as those occurring in fewer than 1/1000 patients) can be found in the manufacturer's package insert.In rasagiline clinical trials, melanoma was noted rarely, but causality is difficult to assign as it appears that Parkinson's disease patients have an increased baseline risk for melanoma. The manufacturer is conducting Phase 4 clinical studies to determine the actual risk of melanoma with the use of rasagiline. Patients should undergo periodic dermatologic examinations to screen for melanoma or precancerous skin lesions.
  Adverse Reactions
    Headline
    General Information
    General Information
    Rasagiline was administered to roughly 1361 patients during Phase 2 and 3 clinical trials. About 283 patients received rasagiline for at least 1 year, approximately 410 patients received rasagiline for at least 2 years, 116 patients received rasagiline for at least 3 years, and 245 patients received rasagiline for more than 3 years. The long-term safety profile appears to be similar to that observed with shorter duration exposure. In general the most common adverse events seen with the use of rasagiline in clinical trials include: loss of weight, orthostatic or postural hypotension, dry mouth, abnormal involuntary movement, and hallucination (see rasagiline below for more detail). Many of these adverse events may be dose-related.
 
In the double-blind, placebo-controlled trials conducted in patients receiving rasagiline as monotherapy, roughly 5% of the patients treated with rasagiline (n=149) discontinued treatment due to adverse events compared to 2% of patients who received placebo (n=151). In studies conducted in patients treated with adjunctive levodopa therapy, approximately 9% of patients treated with rasagiline 0.5 mg/day PO (n=164) and 7% of patients treated with rasagiline 1 mg/day PO (n=149) stopped treatment due to adverse events compared to 6% of the patients who received placebo (n=159). In both the TEMPO and PRESTO clinical trials, serious adverse events occurred more frequently in older (>= 70 years) than younger patients (< 70 years), irrespective of treatment with placebo or rasagiline. Additionally, the manufacturers package labeling states that there are no significant differences in safety based on age. However, older patients may be more prone to adverse events if they have impaired hepatic function (see Dosage).
    Headline
    agitation, anxiety, aphasia, ataxia, delirium, depression, drowsiness, dysarthria, dysesthesia, dyskinesia, dystonic reaction, emotional lability, hallucinations, headache, hostility, hyperesthesia, hyperkinesis, hypertonia, involuntary movements, mania, memory impairment, migraine, myelitis, myoclonia, neuritis, neuropathic pain, paranoia, paresthesias, psychosis, seizures, tremor, vertigo
    General Information
    Central nervous system adverse events may occur with the use of rasagiline. When used as an adjunct to levodopa, rasagiline may potentiate dopaminergic side effects and exacerbate pre-existing dyskinesia, involuntary movements, or hallucinations. During monotherapy clinical trials for Parkinson's disease, the following centrally-mediated effects occurred in at least 2% of patients receiving rasagiline and more frequently than placebo: headache (14% vs 12%), depression (5% vs 2%), paresthesias (2% vs 1%), and vertigo (2% vs 1%). The only adverse event that led to the discontinuation of more than one patient in the monotherapy trials was hallucinations. During clinical trials of rasagiline 0.5—1 mg as adjunct therapy to levodopa, the following CNS effects occurred more frequently in the active treatment group than the placebo group: dyskinesia (18% vs 10%), headache (8—11% vs 10%), drowsiness (4—6% vs 4%), paresthesias (2—5% vs 3%), abnormal dreams (1—4% vs 1%), ataxia (3—6% vs 1%), and dystonic reaction (2—3% vs 1%). CNS effects reported in at least 1% of patients during other clinical trial evaluation included abnormal gait, anxiety, hyperkinesis, hypertonia, neuropathy, and tremor. CNS effects reported in 0.1—1% of patients included agitation, aphasia, circumoral paresthesia, seizures, delusions, dementia (memory impairment), dysarthria, dysautonomia, dysesthesia, emotional lability, facial paralysis, foot drop, hemiplegia, hyperesthesia, incoordination, mania, migraine, myoclonia, neuritis, neurosis, paranoia, personality disorder, psychosis, and wrist drop. Hallucinations were reported in patients treated with: rasagiline monotherapy (1.3% vs 0.7% placebo), rasagiline 1 mg as an adjunct therapy without levodopa (1.2% vs 1.8% placebo), and rasagiline 0.5—1 mg as adjunct therapy to levodopa (4—5% vs 3%). During clinical trials, hallucinations led to drug discontinuation and premature withdrawal in 0.6—1.3% of patients. Post-marketing reports indicate that following initiation of rasagiline therapy or dose escalation, patients may experience new or worsening mental status and behavioral changes, which may be severe, including psychotic-like behavior. Rare effects (< 0.1%) included apathy, delirium, hostility, myelitis, neuropathic pain, psychotic depression, and stupor. Elderly patients may be at greater risk for treatment-emergent adverse psychiatric effects. In patients who develop treatment emergent dyskinesias or hallucinations, the clinician may consider reducing the dose of levodopa and monitoring for effectiveness and reduction of the adverse event.

    Headline
    AV block, bundle-branch block, heart failure, myocardial infarction, orthostatic hypotension, phlebitis, thrombosis, ventricular fibrillation, ventricular tachycardia
    General Information
    Postural hypotension associated with rasagiline therapy is most likely dose-related and may occur more frequently in patients using levodopa as adjunct therapy. There is no clear risk of hypotension or orthostatic hypotension when rasagiline is used as monotherapy. When rasagiline was used as an adjunct therapy to levodopa, with some patients also taking dopamine agonists, COMT inhibitors, anticholinergics, or amantadine, postural hypotension was reported in approximately 13% of patients treated with 1 mg rasagiline and 9% of patients treated with placebo. At the 1 mg dose, the frequency of postural hypotension (at any time during the study) was approximately 44% for rasagiline vs 33% for placebo for mild to moderate systolic blood pressure decrements (more than 20 mmHg), 40% for rasagiline vs 33% for placebo for mild to moderate diastolic blood pressure decrements (more than 10 mmHg), 7% for rasagiline vs 3% for placebo for severe systolic blood pressure decrements (more than 40 mmHg), and 9% for rasagiline vs 6% for placebo for severe diastolic blood pressure decrements (20 mmHg or more). There was also an increased risk for some of these abnormalities at the lower 0.5 mg daily dose and for an individual patient having mild to moderate or severe postural hypotension for both systolic and diastolic blood pressure. When used as an adjunct therapy in patients not taking concomitant levodopa, postural hypotension was reported in 3.1% of patients receiving rasagiline 1 mg and 0.6% of patients receiving placebo. When used as an adjunct to levodopa, postural hypotension was reported in approximately 6% of patients treated with 0.5 mg rasagiline, 9% of patients treated with 1 mg rasagiline and 3% of patients treated with placebo. Postural hypotension led to drug discontinuation and premature withdrawal from clinical trials in one (0.7%) patient treated with rasagiline 1 mg/day, no patients treated with rasagiline 0.5 mg/day and no placebo-treated patients. Clinical trial data suggest that postural hypotension occurs most frequently in the first 2 months of rasagiline treatment and tends to decrease over time. Some patients treated with rasagiline experienced a mildly increased risk for significant decreases in blood pressure unrelated to standing, but while supine. Bundle-branch block was reported in at least 1% of patients during clinical trial evaluation. Adverse cardiovascular effects reported in 0.1 to 1% of patients included deep thrombophlebitis, heart failure, myocardial infarction, phlebitis, and ventricular tachycardia. Rare effects (less than 0.1%) included arterial thrombosis, atrial arrhythmia, complete AV block, second degree AV block, bigeminy, cerebral hemorrhage, cerebral ischemia, and ventricular fibrillation.

    Headline
    abdominal pain, anorexia, colitis, constipation, diarrhea, dyspepsia, esophageal ulceration, esophagitis, fecal incontinence, gastritis, GI bleeding, GI obstruction, GI perforation, gingivitis, hematemesis, jaundice, melena, nausea, oral ulceration, peptic ulcer, stomatitis, toxic megacolon, vomiting, weight loss, xerostomia
    General Information
    During monotherapy clinical trials for Parkinson's disease, the following gastrointestinal (GI) effects occurred in at least 2% of patients receiving rasagiline and more frequently than placebo: dyspepsia (7%) and gastroenteritis (3%). During clinical trials of rasagiline 0.5 mg to 1 mg as adjunct therapy to levodopa, the following GI effects occurred more frequently in the active treatment group than the placebo group: nausea (10% to 12%), weight loss (2% to 9%), constipation (4% to 9%), vomiting (4% to 7%), xerostomia (2% to 6%), abdominal pain (2% to 5%), anorexia (2% to 5%), diarrhea (5% to 7%), dyspepsia (4% to 5%), gingivitis (1% to 2%), and hernia (1% to 2%). GI bleeding was reported in at least 1% of patients during other clinical trial evaluation. GI effects reported in 0.1% to 1% of patients included colitis, esophageal ulceration, esophagitis, fecal incontinence, GI obstruction, oral ulceration, peptic ulcer, stomatitis, and tongue edema. Rare effects (less than  0.1%) included hematemesis, hemorrhagic gastritis, GI perforation, intestinal stenosis, jaundice, large intestine perforation, toxic megacolon, and melena. Rasagiline may be taken with food; however, it is not known if this will help to lessen the incidence of adverse GI events such as nausea or dyspepsia.

    apnea, cough, dyspnea, fever, infection, influenza, laryngeal edema, laryngospasm, pleural effusion, pneumothorax, pulmonary fibrosis, rhinitis
    General Information
    During monotherapy clinical trials for Parkinson's disease, the following adverse respiratory events, infections, or related symptoms occurred in at least 2% of patients receiving rasagiline and more frequently than placebo: influenza (5% vs 1%), fever (3% vs 1%), and rhinitis (3% vs 1%). Increased cough was reported in at least 1% of patients during other clinical trial evaluation. Respiratory effects reported in 0.1—1% of patients included apnea, emphysema, laryngismus (laryngospasm), pleural effusion, and pneumothorax. Rare effects (< 0.1%) included interstitial pneumonia, laryngeal edema, and pulmonary fibrosis.

    General Information

During other clinical trial evaluation, musculoskeletal effects reported in 0.1—1% of patients included osteonecrosis and muscle atrophy. Arthrosis (arthropathy) was reported rarely (< 0.1%).

    Headline
    atopic dermatitis, ecchymosis, exfoliative dermatitis, hyperhidrosis, new primary malignancy, photosensitivity, rash, urticaria
    General Information
    During monotherapy clinical trials for Parkinson's disease, the following dermatologic effect occurred in at least 2% of patients receiving rasagiline and more frequently than placebo: ecchymosis (2%). During clinical trials of rasagiline 0.5 mg to 1 mg as adjunct therapy to levodopa, the following dermatologic effects occurred more frequently in the active treatment group than the placebo group: rash (unspecified) (3% to 6%), ecchymosis (2% to 5%), and hyperhidrosis (2% to 3%). During other clinical trial evaluation, the following dermatologic effects were reported in 0.1% to 1% of patients: atopic dermatitis, urticaria, and photosensitivity. Rare effects (less than 0.1%) included exfoliative dermatitis and leukoderma. New primary malignancy (melanoma) has been reported during postmarketing use; however, causality to the drug has not been established.

    Headline
    hypertension, hypertensive crisis
    General Information
    MAOIs can predispose patients to develop a hypertensive crisis. Rasagiline selectively inhibits MAO type B and at recommended dosages does not affect MAO type A, which is the predominant MAO enzyme that affects catecholamine degradation. Patients taking rasagiline can therefore consume most foods containing tyramine, or medicines containing pharmacologically active amines with less risk of uncontrolled hypertension. The possibility of hypertension may still exist during rasagiline therapy, especially at a dosage greater than the recommended maximum of 1 mg/day. In some studies using concomitant treatment with rasagiline and levodopa, rasagiline produced an increased incidence of significant high blood pressure (e.g., systolic > 180 or diastolic > 100 mm Hg) of 4% versus 3% for placebo. During post-marketing use of rasagiline, rare cases of hypertensive crisis have occurred in patients who ingested unknown quantities of tyramine-rich foods. Therefore, patients should be warned to report severe or continuing headache and any other symptoms of hypertension, such as angina, sinus tachycardia, sinus bradycardia, palpitations, mydriasis, diaphoresis, nausea/vomiting, stiff or sore neck, and fever while receiving rasagiline.

Less frequently occurring hematologic effects reported during clinical trial evaluation of rasagiline included macrocytic anemia (0.1—1%), purpura (< 0.1%), and thrombocythemia (< 0.1%).

    asthenia, chills, edema, flank pain, hypocalcemia, malaise

Other general effects that were reported in 0.1—1% of patients included chills, facial edema, flank pain, and hypocalcemia.

      malaise

      Incidence To
    Headline
    impulse control symptoms
    General Information
    Impulse control symptoms and compulsive behaviors, such as pathological gambling, intense urges to spend money, binge eating, and hypersexuality, have been reported during post-market use of rasagiline. Some patients receiving medications that increase dopaminergic tone have reported intense and uncontrollable urges to gamble, increased sexual urges, or other intense urges. Causality due to dopaminergic agents has not been established; however, in some cases, the urges stopped after the dose was reduced or the drug was discontinued. Practitioners should inquire periodically about new or worsening impulse control symptoms in patients receiving rasagiline. Likewise, patients should be instructed to report such changes while receiving rasagiline. Dose reduction or discontinuation should be considered in those who experience these effects.
    Adverse Reaction GSTerms
      GSTerm Name
      impulse control symptoms
      Severity
      Moderate
    Headline
    serotonin syndrome
    General Information
    Serotonin syndrome has been reported during post-market use of rasagiline in combination with antidepressants. Symptoms of serotonin syndrome may include nausea, vomiting, sedation, dizziness, diaphoresis (sweating), facial flush, mental status changes, myoclonia, restlessness, shivering, and hypertension. If serotonin syndrome becomes evident during treatment, rasagiline and any serotonergic agents should be discontinued and appropriate medical treatment should be initiated.
    Adverse Reaction GSTerms
      GSTerm Name
      serotonin syndrome
      Severity
      Severe
    Headline
    neuroleptic malignant syndrome-like symptoms
    General Information
    Although not reported with rasagiline, neuroleptic malignant syndrome-like symptoms (characterized by elevated temperature, muscular rigidity, altered consciousness, and autonomic instability), with no other obvious etiology, have been reported in association with rapid dose reduction, withdrawal of, or changes in anti-Parkinsonian therapy. Abrupt discontinuation of long-term treatment is not recommended. When possible, the dosage should be reduced gradually to avoid these withdrawal-emergent side effects.
    Adverse Reaction GSTerms
      GSTerm Name
      neuroleptic malignant syndrome-like symptoms
      Severity
      Severe

Description
  Text
  Rasagiline mesylate (Azilect) is a potent, second generation, irreversible monoamine oxidase inhibitor type B (MAO-B). Rasagiline is indicated for the treatment of the signs and symptoms of idiopathic Parkinson's disease as initial monotherapy (in early disease) and as adjunct therapy to levodopa (in later disease). Rasagiline maintains selective monoamine oxidase-B (MAO-B) inhibition at manufacturer recommended doses, although MAO-B selectivity decreases in a dose-related manner as increases are made above the recommended daily dose. Dietary tyramine restrictions are not ordinarily required when established dosing guidelines are followed. However, it should be noted that ingestion of substances with very high amounts of tyramine such as Stilton aged cheese (i.e., > 150 mg) may still result in a hypertensive reaction during rasagiline administration. Rasagiline has been demonstrated to be at least 5 times more potent as an inhibitor of MAO-B than selegiline. Unlike selegiline, which is metabolized to amphetamine derivatives, rasagiline is biotransformed to the nonamphetamine compound aminoindan. Safety and efficacy have been demonstrated in 3 multicenter, 18- to 26-week placebo-controlled clinical trials (n > 1500), including the TEMPO (TVP-1012 in Early Monotherapy for Parkinson's Disease Outpatients), PRESTO (Parkinson's Rasagiline: Efficacy and Safety in the Treatment of Off) and LARGO (Lasting Effect in Adjunct Therapy with Rasagiline Given Once Daily) studies evaluating rasagiline as either mono- or adjunct therapy. Positive effects have been demonstrated on motor impairment and activities of daily living in early and moderate-to-advanced stage patients. Improvements have been noted in the Unified Parkinson's Disease Rating Scale (UPDRS) scores, Clinical Global Improvement (CGI) scores, and amount of levodopa 'off' time (a reduction of roughly 0.5—1 hour) and motor symptom scores. In both the PRESTO and LARGO studies, patients were also on other concomitant Parkinson's disease medications. In the LARGO study, entacapone was included as a comparative group, and differences in safety or efficacy were not noted compared to rasagiline. It appears rasagiline may exhibit a neuroprotective effect via mechanisms such as upregulation of cellular antioxidant activity and antiapoptotic factors, but the true clinical significance of this effect is still under investigation. In a randomized, controlled clinical trial, patients who had a 6-month delay in the start of rasagiline treatment (compared to those who received a full 12-month treatment period) demonstrated more functional decline as measured by the UPDRS. On May 17, 2006 the FDA approved rasagiline (Azilect) as monotherapy for the treatment of early Parkinson's disease and as an adjunct to levodopa in more advanced patients.

Mechanism Of Action
  Text
  Rasagiline is an irreversible monoamine oxidase (MAO) inhibitor indicated for the treatment of idiopathic Parkinson's disease. MAO is classified into two major molecular species, A and B, and is localized in mitochondrial membranes throughout the body in nerve terminals, brain, liver and intestinal mucosa. MAO regulates the metabolic degradation of catecholamines and serotonin in the CNS and peripheral tissues. Blockade of MAO-B reduces the metabolism of dopamine, but not that of norepinephrine or serotonin. Rasagiline inhibits MAO type B (MAO-B), the main form of the MAO enzyme in the human brain. Rasagiline maintains selective monoamine oxidase-B (MAO-B) inhibition at manufacturer recommended doses, although MAO-B selectivity decreases in a dose-related manner as increases are made above the recommended daily dose (see Contraindications). Unlike selegiline, which is metabolized to amphetamine derivatives, rasagiline is biotransformed to the nonamphetamine compound aminoindan.
 
The precise mechanisms of action of rasagiline are unknown. One mechanism is believed to be related to its MAO-B inhibitory activity, which causes an increase in extracellular levels of dopamine in the striatum. The elevated dopamine level and subsequent increased dopaminergic activity are likely to mediate rasagiline's beneficial effects seen in models of dopaminergic motor dysfunction. Studies in healthy subjects and in Parkinson's disease patients have shown that rasagiline inhibits platelet MAO-B irreversibly. The inhibition lasts at least 1 week after last dose. Almost 25—35% MAO-B inhibition was achieved after a single rasagiline dose of 1 mg/day PO and more than 55% of MAO-B inhibition was achieved after a single rasagiline dose of 2 mg/day PO. Over 90% inhibition was achieved 3 days after rasagiline daily dosing at 2 mg/day PO and this inhibition level was maintained 3 days post-dose. Multiple doses of rasagiline of 0.5, 1 and 2 mg/day PO resulted in complete MAO-B inhibition.

Pharmacokinetics
  Route Specific Pharmacokinetics
    Name
    Oral Route
    Description
    Rasagiline is rapidly absorbed from the GI tract, reaching peak plasma concentration (Cmax) in approximately 1 hour. Pharmacokinetic parameters are linear with doses over the range of 1—10 mg. The absolute bioavailability is roughly 36%. Food does not affect the Tmax, although Cmax and exposure (AUC) are decreased by approximately 60% and 20%, respectively, when the drug is taken with a high fat meal. Because AUC is not significantly affected, rasagiline can be administered with or without food.
  Special Populations
    Name
    Hepatic Impairment
    Description
    Rasagiline is extensively metabolized in the liver. Following repeat dose administration in subjects with mild hepatic impairment, the AUC and Cmax were increased by 2 fold and 1.4 fold, respectively, compared to healthy subjects. Patients with mild hepatic impairment will require a dose reduction (see Dosage). In subjects with moderate hepatic impairment, AUC and Cmax were increased by 7 fold and 2 fold, respectively, compared to healthy subjects. Rasagiline should not be used in patients with moderate or severe hepatic impairment.
    Name
    Renal Impairment
    Description
    Conclusive data are not available for renally impaired patients. As unconjugated rasagiline is not excreted by the kidney, it can be given at usual doses in patients with mild renal impairment.
    Name
    Pediatrics
    Description
    Rasagiline pharmacokinetics have not been described in patients below 18 years of age.
    Name
    Geriatric
    Description
    Age as a single risk factor has little influence on the pharmacokinetics of rasagiline, therefore it can be administered at the recommended dose in the elderly assuming liver and kidney function are normal.
  Text
  Rasagiline is administered orally. The mean volume of distribution at steady-state is 87 L, indicating that the tissue binding is in excess of plasma protein binding. Plasma protein binding ranges from 88—94% with mean extent of binding of 61—63% to human albumin over the concentration range of 1—100 ng/ml. The half life is roughly 3 hours but there is no correlation of pharmacokinetics with its pharmacological effect because of irreversible inhibition of MAO-B. Rasagiline undergoes almost complete biotransformation in the liver prior to excretion. The metabolism proceeds through N-dealkylation and/or hydroxylation. In vitro experiments indicate that both routes of metabolism are dependent on the cytochrome P450 (CYP) system, with CYP1A2 being the major isoenzyme involved. Glucuronide conjugation of rasagiline and its metabolites, with subsequent urinary excretion, is the major elimination pathway. Elimination occurs primarily via urine (62%) and secondarily via feces (7%) with a total calculated recovery of 84% of the dose over a period of 38 days. Less than 1% is excreted as unchanged drug in urine.
 
Affected cytochrome P450 isoenzymes and drug transporters: CYP1A2, CYP2C8/9
In vitro metabolism studies have shown that cytochrome P450 (CYP) 1A2 is the major hepatic enzyme responsible for the metabolism of rasagiline. There is the potential for inhibitors of the CYP1A2 enzyme to increase rasagiline plasma concentrations up to 2-fold when coadministered, potentially leading to enhanced adverse effects. In vitro studies have shown that rasagiline at a concentration of 1 mcg/ml (equivalent to a level that is 160 times the average Cmax) did not inhibit cytochrome P450 isoenzymes CYP1A2, CYP2A6, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, CYP2E1, CYP3A4, or CYP4A. These results indicate that rasagiline is unlikely to cause any clinically significant interference with substrates of these enzymes; however, in vivo clinical drug interaction studies have not been completed.

Hazardous Drugs Classification
NIOSH 2024 List: Table 2
Observe and exercise appropriate precautions for handling, preparation, administration, and disposal of hazardous drugs.
Use gloves to handle; double chemotherapy gloves and a protective gown are needed if manipulation is necessary. Cutting, crushing, or otherwise manipulating tablets/capsules will increase exposure.
  Route Specific Administrations
    Name
    Oral Administration
    Description
    Tablets
Administer rasagiline at the same time each day, with or without food.
Missed dose: If a dose is missed, skip the missed dose and administer the next dose at the usual time on the following day.
Food and drug interactions with rasagiline can be serious. Avoid foods/beverages containing large amounts of tyramine.

Dosage Adjustment Guidelines
  Hepatic Impairment
  Mild hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh A; score 5 to 6): Do not exceed a dose of 0.5 mg PO once daily.
Moderate or severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh B or C; score 7 to 15): Do not use.
  Renal Impairment
  Mild to moderate renal impairment: Dosage adjustments are not required.
Severe renal impairment: Data are not available.
